Hi. Thank you. Um, well, I’m here because my sleep has basically just completely fallen apart. I mean, it's just gone. Every single night, I’ll be in my apartment, and I’ll finally force myself to close my laptop for the night, and I'll get into bed, but I'll just lie there for hours. Literally hours. The laptop is physically closed, but my brain is just... it's still wide open and running. 

I'm just lying there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, mentally rechecking every single work deadline I have coming up—because there are always so many moving parts with the projects I'm analyzing—and then that somehow bleeds into stressing about my tuition payments, and then I'm going over my class assignments and wondering if I missed a syllabus update... it's just this constant, exhausting loop that I can't stop. And the absolute worst part is, even on the nights when I do finally manage to fall asleep, I wake up the next morning feeling like I never really turned off at all. Like my brain was just running on a treadmill in the background the entire night. I'm just so incredibly drained from it, and I don't know how to make it stop.

Um, well, if I really think back on it, it’s been... gosh, it’s been about a year and three months now. I know the exact timeline because it lines up perfectly with when I decided to enroll in this evening graduate certificate program. Which, you know, at the time seemed like a really smart, logical step for my career. I work as a management analyst full-time, and I just kept thinking, I need to get ahead, I need to keep upskilling, because in my field, if you aren't constantly proving your value and learning the newest methodologies, you just get left behind. 

So I thought, oh, I can handle this, I can just take evening classes while keeping my full-time job. People do it all the time, right? But I completely underestimated what it would actually look like to juggle both of those massive commitments at the exact same time. So I’m working, you know, forty, sometimes fifty hours a week at the firm, dealing with all these intense data projects and constant client demands, and then the literal second I log off from my job, I’m immediately logging into a virtual lecture or trying to research a paper. 

It was like a switch flipped right around that first month of the program, about fifteen months ago. I just stopped being able to transition from 'work mode' or 'school mode' into 'rest mode.' I remember the exact week it really hit me, it was midterms for my first class, and I also had a huge quarterly report due for my job, and I was just sitting there realizing I had zero buffer time in my life. And I haven't slept normally since. It's just been a year and three months of this non-stop, unrelenting pressure that I guess I put on myself, but I just... I can't seem to turn the dial back down now, no matter how exhausted I get.

Yeah, honestly, yes. All the time. It's like... it doesn't even matter if I actually have something due the next day. The worry is just always there, hovering over me. Even on a Saturday morning, when I technically have absolutely nothing scheduled, no work emails, no class assignments immediately pending, my brain just starts churning. 

It's like my mind just keeps jumping from one catastrophe to the next. I'll be sitting on my couch trying to drink a cup of coffee, and suddenly I'm thinking, "Am I falling behind at work? Did I miss something in that last analysis? What if my manager thinks I'm slipping?" And then, before I can even process that, it pivots right into, "Am I completely wasting my money on this graduate program? Is this certificate even going to matter, or am I just throwing thousands of dollars away?" 

And then it just spirals out from there. I start worrying about my family, you know? Like, what if they see how stressed I am and think I just can't handle things? What if they think I'm failing at being an adult? I literally treat every single small issue, every tiny little hiccup, like it's this massive, catastrophic event that is going to completely derail my entire future. Like if I don't reply to an email perfectly, my career is over. If I don't get an A on a paper, I'm a failure. It's just... it's exhausting because there's no off switch, even when there's nothing actually urgent happening right in front of me.

I can't seem to turn the dial back down now, no matter how exhausted I get.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about things even when there isn’t an immediate deadline or specific task that needs your attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, honestly, yes. Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm physically restless. It's more... it's all in my head, you know? Like, my mind is just racing constantly, but my body is just sort of... there. I don't feel like I need to get up and pace around or anything. I'm usually just sitting there, completely frozen, while my brain goes a million miles an hour. 

I guess sometimes I might tap my foot or something, but it's not this overwhelming urge to move. It's not like I'm jumping out of my skin physically. It's just this intense, paralyzing mental exhaustion. Like, I'll be sitting at my desk, staring at a spreadsheet, and I'm not fidgeting, I'm just completely absorbed in whatever terrible scenario my brain has cooked up for me that day. So, yeah, I wouldn't say I feel physically on edge or restless in that way. It's just the mental part that's so overwhelming. I mean, is that normal? To have all this mental anxiety but not really feel it physically like that? Because I've read about people who get, like, really jittery, and I just don't really get that. I just get stuck in my own head.

Oh my gosh, yes. It's honestly one of the most frustrating parts of all this. I am just so, so tired. And it’s not like a normal tired where you just need a good night's sleep, you know? It’s this deep, heavy kind of exhaustion that just never leaves me. 

Like, every single day, right around midafternoon at the office—maybe 2:30 or 3:00—I just feel completely and utterly drained. I'll be sitting at my desk, looking at my monitors, and I swear, even something as simple as typing out a response to a basic email takes so much effort I almost want to cry. It’s like my brain and my body are just wading through wet cement, and I have to physically force myself to hit the keys. 

And then, at the end of the day, just the walk to the subway... it’s only a few blocks from my building, which used to be nothing, just a quick little walk. But lately, it feels like I'm trekking up a mountain. I have to consciously force myself to just put one foot in front of the other because I have absolutely zero energy left in my body. It takes so much effort just to get home. 

And the worst part is that resting doesn't even fix it. I’ll tell myself, 'Okay, I’m just going to do absolutely nothing this weekend, I'm just going to lay on the couch and recover.' But it doesn't matter. I can sit around all Saturday and Sunday, maybe watch some TV, try to take it easy, and it just doesn't seem to give me my energy back at all. Monday morning rolls around and I feel exactly as depleted as I did on Friday afternoon. It makes no sense to me because, exactly like you said, I'm not doing anything physically demanding! I'm an analyst, I sit in a chair all day! I shouldn't be this bone-tired. But I just feel so heavy and wiped out all the time, no matter what I do.

No, I wouldn't say I've lost interest at all. Actually, it's almost the opposite? Like, I still really, really want to do all the things I used to do. I haven't stopped liking them or anything. For example, I've always loved reading, and I still buy novels all the time—I have this huge, towering stack of books on my nightstand right now that I genuinely want to get through. So the actual interest, the desire to do these things? That hasn't gone away even a little bit. I still love my hobbies, I still love my friends. It's just... well, like I was just saying about the exhaustion, sometimes I just physically cannot bring myself to go to the dinner, or I'm just too wrapped up in my own head worrying about work to actually focus on the book I'm trying to read. But I still want to read it. I still want to be at the dinner. 

I definitely haven't stopped caring about the things I enjoy. If anything, I get incredibly frustrated with myself because I want to do all these fun things and I just... I just end up sitting there thinking about my spreadsheets instead. Or I'll go to the dinner and I'm physically there, but mentally I'm just worrying about whether I sent a file to the wrong client. But yeah, no, the interest itself is definitely still there. I know some people just totally stop caring about stuff when they get overwhelmed, but I definitely still care. I care a lot. Probably too much, honestly.

I'm just so incredibly drained from it, and I don't know how to make it stop.\n\nDoctor: When did these sleep problems and feelings of constant worry start? I know the exact timeline because it lines up perfectly with when I decided to enroll in this evening graduate certificate program. Which, you know, at the time seemed like a really smart, logical step for my career. I work as a management analyst full-time, and I just kept thinking, I need to get ahead, I need to keep upskilling, because in my field, if you aren't constantly proving your value and learning the newest methodologies, you just get left behind. \n\nSo I thought, oh, I can handle this, I can just take evening classes while keeping my full-time job. People do it all the time, right? But I completely underestimated what it would actually look like to juggle both of those massive commitments at the exact same time. I just stopped being able to transition from 'work mode' or 'school mode' into 'rest mode.' I remember the exact week it really hit me, it was midterms for my first class, and I also had a huge quarterly report due for my job, and I was just sitting there realizing I had zero buffer time in my life. And I haven't slept normally since. It's just been a year and three months of this non-stop, unrelenting pressure that I guess I put on myself, but I just... I can't seem to turn the dial back down now, no matter how exhausted I get.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about things even when there isn’t an immediate deadline or specific task that needs your attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, honestly, yes. All the time. It's like... it doesn't even matter if I actually have something due the next day. The worry is just always there, hovering over me. Even on a Saturday morning, when I technically have absolutely nothing scheduled, no work emails, no class assignments immediately pending, my brain just starts churning. \n\nIt's like my mind just keeps jumping from one catastrophe to the next. I'll be sitting on my couch trying to drink a cup of coffee, and suddenly I'm thinking, \"Am I falling behind at work? Did I miss something in that last analysis? What if my manager thinks I'm slipping?\" And then, before I can even process that, it pivots right into, \"Am I completely wasting my money on this graduate program? Is this certificate even going to matter, or am I just throwing thousands of dollars away?\" \n\nAnd then it just spirals out from there. I start worrying about my family, you know? Like, what if they see how stressed I am and think I just can't handle things? What if they think I'm failing at being an adult? I literally treat every single small issue, every tiny little hiccup, like it's this massive, catastrophic event that is going to completely derail my entire future. Like if I don't reply to an email perfectly, my career is over. If I don't get an A on a paper, I'm a failure. It's just... it's exhausting because there's no off switch, even when there's nothing actually urgent happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re feeling this constant worry, do you also notice any physical restlessness, like an urge to move, fidgeting, or feeling on edge physically?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm physically restless. It's more... it's all in my head, you know? Like, my mind is just racing constantly, but my body is just sort of... there. I don't feel like I need to get up and pace around or anything. I'm usually just sitting there, completely frozen, while my brain goes a million miles an hour. \n\nI guess sometimes I might tap my foot or something, but it's not this overwhelming urge to move. It's not like I'm jumping out of my skin physically. It's just this intense, paralyzing mental exhaustion. Like, I'll be sitting at my desk, staring at a spreadsheet, and I'm not fidgeting, I'm just completely absorbed in whatever terrible scenario my brain has cooked up for me that day. So, yeah, I wouldn't say I feel physically on edge or restless in that way. It's just the mental part that's so overwhelming. I mean, is that normal? To have all this mental anxiety but not really feel it physically like that? Because I've read about people who get, like, really jittery, and I just don't really get that. I just get stuck in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year and a half, have you often felt fatigued or had a lack of energy nearly every day, even when you haven’t been physically active?\n\nPatient: Oh my gosh, yes. It's honestly one of the most frustrating parts of all this. I am just so, so tired. And it’s not like a normal tired where you just need a good night's sleep, you know? It’s this deep, heavy kind of exhaustion that just never leaves me. \n\nLike, every single day, right around midafternoon at the office—maybe 2:30 or 3:00—I just feel completely and utterly drained. I'll be sitting at my desk, looking at my monitors, and I swear, even something as simple as typing out a response to a basic email takes so much effort I almost want to cry. It’s like my brain and my body are just wading through wet cement, and I have to physically force myself to hit the keys. \n\nAnd then, at the end of the day, just the walk to the subway... it’s only a few blocks from my building, which used to be nothing, just a quick little walk. But lately, it feels like I'm trekking up a mountain. I have to consciously force myself to just put one foot in front of the other because I have absolutely zero energy left in my body. It takes so much effort just to get home. \n\nAnd the worst part is that resting doesn't even fix it. I’ll tell myself, 'Okay, I’m just going to do absolutely nothing this weekend, I'm just going to lay on the couch and recover.' But it doesn't matter. I can sit around all Saturday and Sunday, maybe watch some TV, try to take it easy, and it just doesn't seem to give me my energy back at all. Monday morning rolls around and I feel exactly as depleted as I did on Friday afternoon. It makes no sense to me because, exactly like you said, I'm not doing anything physically demanding! I'm an analyst, I sit in a chair all day! I shouldn't be this bone-tired. But I just feel so heavy and wiped out all the time, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year and a half, have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, socializing, or other activities?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've lost interest at all. For example, I've always loved reading, and I still buy novels all the time—I have this huge, towering stack of books on my nightstand right now that I genuinely want to get through. And with my friends, you know, they'll invite me out to dinner or to grab drinks after work on a Friday, and I genuinely want to go. I'll be looking forward to it all week, I'll even look up the restaurant menu online on like, a Tuesday, and figure out what I want to order, because I love trying new places. \n\nSo the actual interest, the desire to do these things? That hasn't gone away even a little bit. I still love my hobbies, I still love my friends. It's just... well, like I was just saying about the exhaustion, sometimes I just physically cannot bring myself to go to the dinner, or I'm just too wrapped up in my own head worrying about work to actually focus on the book I'm trying to read. But I still want to read it. I still want to be at the dinner. \n\nI definitely haven't stopped caring about the things I enjoy. If anything, I get incredibly frustrated with myself because I want to do all these fun things and I just... I just end up sitting there thinking about my spreadsheets instead. Or I'll go to the dinner and I'm physically there, but mentally I'm just worrying about whether I sent a file to the wrong client. But yeah, no, the interest itself is definitely still there. Oh, absolutely. It is... it’s honestly ruining my productivity. I was actually just thinking about this because it’s been such a massive issue at work. You know, as a management analyst, my entire job revolves around details and data, so I need to be dialed in. But for the last year and a half, it’s like my brain just absolutely refuses to cooperate, no matter how hard I try to force it. 

I’ll be sitting there, staring at the exact same financial model for ages. Or, you know, even if I'm doing some professional development or a class reading to keep up with industry trends, I will literally read the exact same paragraph five times. Five times! And I'll get to the bottom of the page and realize I haven't absorbed a single word of it. It’s not that the material is too hard or that I don't want to learn it, it’s just that my eyes are just mindlessly skimming the text while my head is already off running through everything that could possibly go wrong. 

Like, I'll be looking at a spreadsheet, and instead of actually processing the numbers in front of me, my brain is spiraling about whether I messed up a formula on a completely different tab, or if the client is going to find a mistake and yell at my boss, or if I'm going to get fired because I missed some tiny, obscure detail. And before I know it, twenty minutes have passed and I'm just frozen, staring blankly at my monitor with a racing heart. 

It is so incredibly frustrating because I really, genuinely want to focus. I try so hard. I'll even close all my other browser tabs, put my phone in a drawer, get a fresh cup of coffee, and tell myself I am going to just buckle down and get this one model done. But it doesn't matter. The worry just completely hijacks my attention every single time. I have to physically shake my head to snap out of it, and then I have to start the whole paragraph or the whole spreadsheet over again from scratch. It makes every single task take three times as long as it used to, which of course just makes me worry more about falling behind on my deadlines, and then I can't concentrate even more because I'm stressing about the time I'm wasting. It’s just this endless, exhausting loop that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I want to just sit and do my work.

It's not like I'm jumping out of my skin physically. It's just this intense, paralyzing mental exhaustion. Like, I'll be sitting at my desk, staring at a spreadsheet, and I'm not fidgeting, I'm just completely absorbed in whatever terrible scenario my brain has cooked up for me that day. So, yeah, I wouldn't say I feel physically on edge or restless in that way. It's just the mental part that's so overwhelming. I mean, is that normal? To have all this mental anxiety but not really feel it physically like that? Because I've read about people who get, like, really jittery, and I just don't really get that. I just get stuck in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year and a half, have you often felt fatigued or had a lack of energy nearly every day, even when you haven’t been physically active?\n\nPatient: Oh my gosh, yes. It's honestly one of the most frustrating parts of all this. I am just so, so tired. And it’s not like a normal tired where you just need a good night's sleep, you know? It’s this deep, heavy kind of exhaustion that just never leaves me. \n\nLike, every single day, right around midafternoon at the office—maybe 2:30 or 3:00—I just feel completely and utterly drained. I'll be sitting at my desk, looking at my monitors, and I swear, even something as simple as typing out a response to a basic email takes so much effort I almost want to cry. It’s like my brain and my body are just wading through wet cement, and I have to physically force myself to hit the keys. \n\nAnd then, at the end of the day, just the walk to the subway... it’s only a few blocks from my building, which used to be nothing, just a quick little walk. But lately, it feels like I'm trekking up a mountain. I have to consciously force myself to just put one foot in front of the other because I have absolutely zero energy left in my body. It takes so much effort just to get home. \n\nAnd the worst part is that resting doesn't even fix it. I’ll tell myself, 'Okay, I’m just going to do absolutely nothing this weekend, I'm just going to lay on the couch and recover.' But it doesn't matter. I can sit around all Saturday and Sunday, maybe watch some TV, try to take it easy, and it just doesn't seem to give me my energy back at all. Monday morning rolls around and I feel exactly as depleted as I did on Friday afternoon. It makes no sense to me because, exactly like you said, I'm not doing anything physically demanding! I'm an analyst, I sit in a chair all day! I shouldn't be this bone-tired. But I just feel so heavy and wiped out all the time, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year and a half, have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, socializing, or other activities?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've lost interest at all. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Irritable? Um... no, actually, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed that. I mean, I know a lot of people get really snappy or short-tempered when they have a lot on their plate, but that’s just not really how I react to things. I don't find myself snapping at my coworkers or, you know, getting mad at the barista if my coffee order is wrong or anything like that. 

If anything, I think I’m almost the exact opposite? Like, I’m just too... I don't know, I'm too busy worrying about everything to actually be mad at anyone else. Take my commute, for instance. You know how the subway is always getting delayed, and everyone in the car gets so angry and they're sighing and rolling their eyes and complaining loudly? When the train just stops randomly in the middle of a tunnel, I don't get annoyed at all. I don't sit there thinking, "Ugh, this is so irritating, I hate this." Instead, I just immediately start spiraling into total panic. I'll just sit there gripping my bag, thinking, "Oh my god, what if I'm ten minutes late and I miss the start of the morning briefing, and my manager thinks I don't care about my job, and then they start looking at my recent reports and find a mistake..." It’s all just fear, not anger.

Or even at work, if someone on my team messes up a data pull that I really needed for a model, and it sets my whole schedule back. I don't get irritable with them. I don't snap. I just internalize the whole thing and think, "Well, I must not have explained the parameters clearly enough, this is my fault, and now I have to fix it, and if I don't fix it right this second the whole project is going to collapse and the client will drop us." So yeah, I'm not really annoyed with *them* or the situation. I just get completely overwhelmed. 

I mean, like I was saying earlier, I definitely get frustrated with *myself* when I can't focus on a paragraph or when I'm too tired to go out to dinner, but I'm definitely not walking around with a short fuse with other people. I honestly just don't think I have the capacity to be angry or annoyed. It takes energy to be irritable, right? And I'm just so depleted and on edge all the time instead. So, no, I wouldn't say I've been irritable.

And the absolute worst part is, even on the nights when I do finally manage to fall asleep, I wake up the next morning feeling like I never really turned off at all. Like my brain was just running on a treadmill in the background the entire night. I'm just so incredibly drained from it, and I don't know how to make it stop.\n\nDoctor: When did these sleep problems and feelings of constant worry start? Was there a specific event or stressor that seemed to trigger them?\n\nPatient: Um, well, if I really think back on it, it’s been... gosh, it’s been about a year and three months now. I know the exact timeline because it lines up perfectly with when I decided to enroll in this evening graduate certificate program. Which, you know, at the time seemed like a really smart, logical step for my career. I work as a management analyst full-time, and I just kept thinking, I need to get ahead, I need to keep upskilling, because in my field, if you aren't constantly proving your value and learning the newest methodologies, you just get left behind. \n\nSo I thought, oh, I can handle this, I can just take evening classes while keeping my full-time job. People do it all the time, right? But I completely underestimated what it would actually look like to juggle both of those massive commitments at the exact same time. So I’m working, you know, forty, sometimes fifty hours a week at the firm, dealing with all these intense data projects and constant client demands, and then the literal second I log off from my job, I’m immediately logging into a virtual lecture or trying to research a paper. \n\nIt was like a switch flipped right around that first month of the program, about fifteen months ago. I just stopped being able to transition from 'work mode' or 'school mode' into 'rest mode.' I remember the exact week it really hit me, it was midterms for my first class, and I also had a huge quarterly report due for my job, and I was just sitting there realizing I had zero buffer time in my life. And I haven't slept normally since. It's just been a year and three months of this non-stop, unrelenting pressure that I guess I put on myself, but I just... I can't seem to turn the dial back down now, no matter how exhausted I get.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about things even when there isn’t an immediate deadline or specific task that needs your attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, honestly, yes. All the time. It's like... it doesn't even matter if I actually have something due the next day. The worry is just always there, hovering over me. Even on a Saturday morning, when I technically have absolutely nothing scheduled, no work emails, no class assignments immediately pending, my brain just starts churning. \n\nIt's like my mind just keeps jumping from one catastrophe to the next. I'll be sitting on my couch trying to drink a cup of coffee, and suddenly I'm thinking, \"Am I falling behind at work? Did I miss something in that last analysis? What if my manager thinks I'm slipping?\" And then, before I can even process that, it pivots right into, \"Am I completely wasting my money on this graduate program? Is this certificate even going to matter, or am I just throwing thousands of dollars away?\" \n\nAnd then it just spirals out from there. I start worrying about my family, you know? Like, what if they see how stressed I am and think I just can't handle things? What if they think I'm failing at being an adult? I literally treat every single small issue, every tiny little hiccup, like it's this massive, catastrophic event that is going to completely derail my entire future. Like if I don't reply to an email perfectly, my career is over. If I don't get an A on a paper, I'm a failure. It's just... it's exhausting because there's no off switch, even when there's nothing actually urgent happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re feeling this constant worry, do you also notice any physical restlessness, like an urge to move, fidgeting, or feeling on edge physically?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm physically restless. It's more... it's all in my head, you know? Like, my mind is just racing constantly, but my body is just sort of... there. I don't feel like I need to get up and pace around or anything. I'm usually just sitting there, completely frozen, while my brain goes a million miles an hour. \n\nI guess sometimes I might tap my foot or something, but it's not this overwhelming urge to move. It's not like I'm jumping out of my skin physically. It's just this intense, paralyzing mental exhaustion. Like, I'll be sitting at my desk, staring at a spreadsheet, and I'm not fidgeting, I'm just completely absorbed in whatever terrible scenario my brain has cooked up for me that day. So, yeah, I wouldn't say I feel physically on edge or restless in that way. It's just the mental part that's so overwhelming. I mean, is that normal? To have all this mental anxiety but not really feel it physically like that? Because I've read about people who get, like, really jittery, and I just don't really get that. I just get stuck in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year and a half, have you often felt fatigued or had a lack of energy nearly every day, even when you haven’t been physically active?\n\nPatient: Oh my gosh, yes. It's honestly one of the most frustrating parts of all this. I am just so, so tired. And it’s not like a normal tired where you just need a good night's sleep, you know? It’s this deep, heavy kind of exhaustion that just never leaves me. \n\nLike, every single day, right around midafternoon at the office—maybe 2:30 or 3:00—I just feel completely and utterly drained. I'll be sitting at my desk, looking at my monitors, and I swear, even something as simple as typing out a response to a basic email takes so much effort I almost want to cry. It’s like my brain and my body are just wading through wet cement, and I have to physically force myself to hit the keys. \n\nAnd then, at the end of the day, just the walk to the subway... it’s only a few blocks from my building, which used to be nothing, just a quick little walk. But lately, it feels like I'm trekking up a mountain. I have to consciously force myself to just put one foot in front of the other because I have absolutely zero energy left in my body. It takes so much effort just to get home. \n\nAnd the worst part is that resting doesn't even fix it. I’ll tell myself, 'Okay, I’m just going to do absolutely nothing this weekend, I'm just going to lay on the couch and recover.' But it doesn't matter. I can sit around all Saturday and Sunday, maybe watch some TV, try to take it easy, and it just doesn't seem to give me my energy back at all. Monday morning rolls around and I feel exactly as depleted as I did on Friday afternoon. It makes no sense to me because, exactly like you said, I'm not doing anything physically demanding! I'm an analyst, I sit in a chair all day! I shouldn't be this bone-tired. But I just feel so heavy and wiped out all the time, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year and a half, have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, socializing, or other activities?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've lost interest at all. Actually, it's almost the opposite? Like, I still really, really want to do all the things I used to do. I haven't stopped liking them or anything. For example, I've always loved reading, and I still buy novels all the time—I have this huge, towering stack of books on my nightstand right now that I genuinely want to get through. And with my friends, you know, they'll invite me out to dinner or to grab drinks after work on a Friday, and I genuinely want to go. I'll be looking forward to it all week, I'll even look up the restaurant menu online on like, a Tuesday, and figure out what I want to order, because I love trying new places. \n\nSo the actual interest, the desire to do these things? That hasn't gone away even a little bit. I still love my hobbies, I still love my friends. It's just... well, like I was just saying about the exhaustion, sometimes I just physically cannot bring myself to go to the dinner, or I'm just too wrapped up in my own head worrying about work to actually focus on the book I'm trying to read. But I still want to read it. I still want to be at the dinner. \n\nI definitely haven't stopped caring about the things I enjoy. If anything, I get incredibly frustrated with myself because I want to do all these fun things and I just... I just end up sitting there thinking about my spreadsheets instead. Or I'll go to the dinner and I'm physically there, but mentally I'm just worrying about whether I sent a file to the wrong client. But yeah, no, the interest itself is definitely still there. I know some people just totally stop caring about stuff when they get overwhelmed, but I definitely still care. I care a lot. Probably too much, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year and a half, have you had trouble concentrating on tasks, even when you wanted to focus?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It is... it’s honestly ruining my productivity. I was actually just thinking about this because it’s been such a massive issue at work. You know, as a management analyst, my entire job revolves around details and data, so I need to be dialed in. But for the last year and a half, it’s like my brain just absolutely refuses to cooperate, no matter how hard I try to force it. \n\nI’ll be sitting there, staring at the exact same financial model for ages. Or, you know, even if I'm doing some professional development or a class reading to keep up with industry trends, I will literally read the exact same paragraph five times. Five times! And I'll get to the bottom of the page and realize I haven't absorbed a single word of it. It’s not that the material is too hard or that I don't want to learn it, it’s just that my eyes are just mindlessly skimming the text while my head is already off running through everything that could possibly go wrong. \n\nLike, I'll be looking at a spreadsheet, and instead of actually processing the numbers in front of me, my brain is spiraling about whether I messed up a formula on a completely different tab, or if the client is going to find a mistake and yell at my boss, or if I'm going to get fired because I missed some tiny, obscure detail. And before I know it, twenty minutes have passed and I'm just frozen, staring blankly at my monitor with a racing heart. \n\nIt is so incredibly frustrating because I really, genuinely want to focus. I try so hard. I'll even close all my other browser tabs, put my phone in a drawer, get a fresh cup of coffee, and tell myself I am going to just buckle down and get this one model done. But it doesn't matter. The worry just completely hijacks my attention every single time. I have to physically shake my head to snap out of it, and then I have to start the whole paragraph or the whole spreadsheet over again from scratch. It makes every single task take three times as long as it used to, which of course just makes me worry more about falling behind on my deadlines, and then I can't concentrate even more because I'm stressing about the time I'm wasting. It’s just this endless, exhausting loop that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I want to just sit and do my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming more irritable or easily annoyed, especially in situations where you used to be able to stay calm and patient?\n\nPatient: Irritable? Um... no, actually, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed that. I mean, I know a lot of people get really snappy or short-tempered when they have a lot on their plate, but that’s just not really how I react to things. I don't find myself snapping at my coworkers or, you know, getting mad at the barista if my coffee order is wrong or anything like that. \n\nIf anything, I think I’m almost the exact opposite? Like, I’m just too... I don't know, I'm too busy worrying about everything to actually be mad at anyone else. Take my commute, for instance. You know how the subway is always getting delayed, and everyone in the car gets so angry and they're sighing and rolling their eyes and complaining loudly? When the train just stops randomly in the middle of a tunnel, I don't get annoyed at all. I don't sit there thinking, \"Ugh, this is so irritating, I hate this.\" Instead, I just immediately start spiraling into total panic. I'll just sit there gripping my bag, thinking, \"Oh my god, what if I'm ten minutes late and I miss the start of the morning briefing, and my manager thinks I don't care about my job, and then they start looking at my recent reports and find a mistake...\" It’s all just fear, not anger.\n\nOr even at work, if someone on my team messes up a data pull that I really needed for a model, and it sets my whole schedule back. I don't get irritable with them. I don't snap. I just internalize the whole thing and think, \"Well, I must not have explained the parameters clearly enough, this is my fault, and now I have to fix it, and if I don't fix it right this second the whole project is going to collapse and the client will drop us.\" So yeah, I'm not really annoyed with *them* or the situation. I just get completely overwhelmed. \n\nI mean, like I was saying earlier, I definitely get frustrated with *myself* when I can't focus on a paragraph or when I'm too tired to go out to dinner, but I'm definitely not walking around with a short fuse with other people. I honestly just don't think I have the capacity to be angry or annoyed. It takes energy to be irritable, right? And I'm just so depleted and on edge all the time instead. So, no, I wouldn't say I've been irritable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Down or sad? Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't think so. It's actually kind of the opposite of hopeless, if you think about it? Because, to me anyway, hopeless implies that you've just totally given up and you think nothing matters. And my problem is that *everything* matters. Every single tiny little detail matters so much that it's honestly terrifying. 

I don't wake up feeling empty or, you know, heavy and sad. When I wake up, my mind is already going a million miles an hour about the three different client meetings I have that day, and I'm stressing over whether my manager is going to bring up some minor data discrepancy from a week ago that I might have missed. So it's not a sad feeling at all. It's more like... I'm just constantly braced for impact. 

I mean, don't get me wrong, I do cry sometimes. I'll definitely admit that. But it's never because I'm feeling down or depressed. It's strictly out of just... sheer overwhelm. Like, a couple of weeks ago, I actually burst into tears in my car in the office parking garage before going inside. But I wasn't crying because I was sad! I was crying because I had triple-checked this massive quarterly report the night before and I was absolutely convinced I had still missed some catastrophic error that was going to get me fired as soon as I walked through the doors. They were totally panic tears, not sad tears. 

So, no, I don't walk around feeling sad or hopeless. I'm just so desperately trying to keep all the plates spinning and make sure nothing goes wrong, which is exhausting, obviously, like I was saying before, but it's definitely a totally different feeling than being depressed. I care way too much to be hopeless.

But for the last year and a half, it’s like my brain just absolutely refuses to cooperate, no matter how hard I try to force it. \n\nI’ll be sitting there, staring at the exact same financial model for ages. Or, you know, even if I'm doing some professional development or a class reading to keep up with industry trends, I will literally read the exact same paragraph five times. Five times! And I'll get to the bottom of the page and realize I haven't absorbed a single word of it. It’s not that the material is too hard or that I don't want to learn it, it’s just that my eyes are just mindlessly skimming the text while my head is already off running through everything that could possibly go wrong. \n\nLike, I'll be looking at a spreadsheet, and instead of actually processing the numbers in front of me, my brain is spiraling about whether I messed up a formula on a completely different tab, or if the client is going to find a mistake and yell at my boss, or if I'm going to get fired because I missed some tiny, obscure detail. And before I know it, twenty minutes have passed and I'm just frozen, staring blankly at my monitor with a racing heart. \n\nIt is so incredibly frustrating because I really, genuinely want to focus. I try so hard. I'll even close all my other browser tabs, put my phone in a drawer, get a fresh cup of coffee, and tell myself I am going to just buckle down and get this one model done. But it doesn't matter. The worry just completely hijacks my attention every single time. I have to physically shake my head to snap out of it, and then I have to start the whole paragraph or the whole spreadsheet over again from scratch. It makes every single task take three times as long as it used to, which of course just makes me worry more about falling behind on my deadlines, and then I can't concentrate even more because I'm stressing about the time I'm wasting. It’s just this endless, exhausting loop that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I want to just sit and do my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming more irritable or easily annoyed, especially in situations where you used to be able to stay calm and patient?\n\nPatient: Irritable? Um... no, actually, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed that. I mean, I know a lot of people get really snappy or short-tempered when they have a lot on their plate, but that’s just not really how I react to things. I don't find myself snapping at my coworkers or, you know, getting mad at the barista if my coffee order is wrong or anything like that. \n\nIf anything, I think I’m almost the exact opposite? Like, I’m just too... I don't know, I'm too busy worrying about everything to actually be mad at anyone else. Take my commute, for instance. You know how the subway is always getting delayed, and everyone in the car gets so angry and they're sighing and rolling their eyes and complaining loudly? When the train just stops randomly in the middle of a tunnel, I don't get annoyed at all. I don't sit there thinking, \"Ugh, this is so irritating, I hate this.\" Instead, I just immediately start spiraling into total panic. I'll just sit there gripping my bag, thinking, \"Oh my god, what if I'm ten minutes late and I miss the start of the morning briefing, and my manager thinks I don't care about my job, and then they start looking at my recent reports and find a mistake...\" It’s all just fear, not anger.\n\nOr even at work, if someone on my team messes up a data pull that I really needed for a model, and it sets my whole schedule back. I don't get irritable with them. I don't snap. I just internalize the whole thing and think, \"Well, I must not have explained the parameters clearly enough, this is my fault, and now I have to fix it, and if I don't fix it right this second the whole project is going to collapse and the client will drop us.\" So yeah, I'm not really annoyed with *them* or the situation. I just get completely overwhelmed. \n\nI mean, like I was saying earlier, I definitely get frustrated with *myself* when I can't focus on a paragraph or when I'm too tired to go out to dinner, but I'm definitely not walking around with a short fuse with other people. I honestly just don't think I have the capacity to be angry or annoyed. It takes energy to be irritable, right? And I'm just so depleted and on edge all the time instead. So, no, I wouldn't say I've been irritable.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year and a half, have you felt down, sad, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Down or sad? Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't think so. It's actually kind of the opposite of hopeless, if you think about it? Because, to me anyway, hopeless implies that you've just totally given up and you think nothing matters. And my problem is that *everything* matters. Every single tiny little detail matters so much that it's honestly terrifying. \n\nI don't wake up feeling empty or, you know, heavy and sad. When I wake up, my mind is already going a million miles an hour about the three different client meetings I have that day, and I'm stressing over whether my manager is going to bring up some minor data discrepancy from a week ago that I might have missed. So it's not a sad feeling at all. It's more like... I'm just constantly braced for impact. \n\nI mean, don't get me wrong, I do cry sometimes. I'll definitely admit that. But it's never because I'm feeling down or depressed. It's strictly out of just... sheer overwhelm. Like, a couple of weeks ago, I actually burst into tears in my car in the office parking garage before going inside. But I wasn't crying because I was sad! I was crying because I had triple-checked this massive quarterly report the night before and I was absolutely convinced I had still missed some catastrophic error that was going to get me fired as soon as I walked through the doors. They were totally panic tears, not sad tears. \n\nSo, no, I don't walk around feeling sad or hopeless. I'm just so desperately trying to keep all the plates spinning and make sure nothing goes wrong, which is exhausting, obviously, like I was saying before, but it's definitely a totally different feeling than being depressed. I care way too much to be hopeless.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, constantly. It is... it’s just this never-ending carousel in my head, all day, every day. I mean, it’s exactly like you said—there usually isn't even a real, logical reason for me to be panicking, but my mind just completely runs away with things anyway. 

It’s like my brain just keeps jumping from one worst-case scenario to the next without giving me a second to breathe. Like, I’ll start out worrying about whether I’m falling behind at work—which, you know, I'm always terrified of, even if my manager literally just told me I’m doing a great job and I'm totally on schedule. But I can't just accept that. Instead, my brain immediately pivots, and suddenly I'm thinking, "Well, if I'm struggling to keep up with work, then why am I spending all this time and money on these extra professional development courses?" And then I'm spiraling about whether I’m just completely wasting my money on school, and how I'm going to ruin my finances, and what if I can't pay my rent. 

And then, before I can even try to rationalize *that*, I’m suddenly stressing about my family. I'll start thinking about how if I mess up at work and waste all my savings, my parents are going to find out and they're going to think I just completely lack the ability to handle my own life. I'll picture them looking at me with this intense disappointment, thinking I'm this helpless adult who can't handle basic responsibilities—even though they have literally never said anything like that to me, ever! They're actually really supportive, but my brain just invents this narrative where they think I'm a total failure. 

It’s honestly exhausting because it happens with everything. It’s not just the big stuff. It’s like... I treat every single small, insignificant issue like it is this massive, catastrophic event that could completely derail my whole future. An unread email, a slightly weird tone in a text message, a minor typo in a draft—I somehow twist it in my head until it means I'm going to lose my job, go broke, and alienate my entire family. I just can't seem to stop connecting these tiny, everyday things to the absolute ruin of my life, no matter how much I try to tell myself I'm being irrational.

Because, to me anyway, hopeless implies that you've just totally given up and you think nothing matters. And my problem is that *everything* matters. Every single tiny little detail matters so much that it's honestly terrifying. \n\nI don't wake up feeling empty or, you know, heavy and sad. When I wake up, my mind is already going a million miles an hour about the three different client meetings I have that day, and I'm stressing over whether my manager is going to bring up some minor data discrepancy from a week ago that I might have missed. So it's not a sad feeling at all. It's more like... I'm just constantly braced for impact. \n\nI mean, don't get me wrong, I do cry sometimes. I'll definitely admit that. But it's never because I'm feeling down or depressed. It's strictly out of just... sheer overwhelm. Like, a couple of weeks ago, I actually burst into tears in my car in the office parking garage before going inside. But I wasn't crying because I was sad! I was crying because I had triple-checked this massive quarterly report the night before and I was absolutely convinced I had still missed some catastrophic error that was going to get me fired as soon as I walked through the doors. They were totally panic tears, not sad tears. \n\nSo, no, I don't walk around feeling sad or hopeless. I'm just so desperately trying to keep all the plates spinning and make sure nothing goes wrong, which is exhausting, obviously, like I was saying before, but it's definitely a totally different feeling than being depressed. I care way too much to be hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a variety of everyday things, even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned about them?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It is... it’s just this never-ending carousel in my head, all day, every day. I mean, it’s exactly like you said—there usually isn't even a real, logical reason for me to be panicking, but my mind just completely runs away with things anyway. \n\nIt’s like my brain just keeps jumping from one worst-case scenario to the next without giving me a second to breathe. Like, I’ll start out worrying about whether I’m falling behind at work—which, you know, I'm always terrified of, even if my manager literally just told me I’m doing a great job and I'm totally on schedule. But I can't just accept that. Instead, my brain immediately pivots, and suddenly I'm thinking, \"Well, if I'm struggling to keep up with work, then why am I spending all this time and money on these extra professional development courses?\" And then I'm spiraling about whether I’m just completely wasting my money on school, and how I'm going to ruin my finances, and what if I can't pay my rent. \n\nAnd then, before I can even try to rationalize *that*, I’m suddenly stressing about my family. I'll start thinking about how if I mess up at work and waste all my savings, my parents are going to find out and they're going to think I just completely lack the ability to handle my own life. I'll picture them looking at me with this intense disappointment, thinking I'm this helpless adult who can't handle basic responsibilities—even though they have literally never said anything like that to me, ever! They're actually really supportive, but my brain just invents this narrative where they think I'm a total failure. \n\nIt’s honestly exhausting because it happens with everything. It’s not just the big stuff. It’s like... I treat every single small, insignificant issue like it is this massive, catastrophic event that could completely derail my whole future. An unread email, a slightly weird tone in a text message, a minor typo in a draft—I somehow twist it in my head until it means I'm going to lose my job, go broke, and alienate my entire family. I just can't seem to stop connecting these tiny, everyday things to the absolute ruin of my life, no matter how much I try to tell myself I'm being irrational.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Sudden episodes? Um... no, I wouldn't say they're sudden. And they definitely don't just peak in a few minutes and then go away. It's really not like that for me at all. 

I think I know exactly what you're talking about, though, because I had a roommate in college who used to get those. She would just suddenly be terrified out of nowhere, and her heart would be racing so fast she thought she was having a heart attack, and she'd be sweating and hyperventilating. I would have to sit with her on the floor and help her count her breaths until it passed. But I don't experience anything like that. My fear isn't this sudden, out-of-the-blue attack that hits me like a wave and then recedes. It's more like... it's just always there. It's a constant, steady hum of dread that starts the second I open my eyes in the morning and doesn't stop until I finally manage to fall asleep. 

So I don't get those sudden physical things you mentioned, like the sweating or the shortness of breath or the trembling. I mean, sure, my heart might beat a little fast sometimes when I'm really, really spiraling about a project deadline or if I have to present a big data analysis to the senior management team, but it's not like a sudden, intense spike where I can't catch my breath or anything. It's just this continuous, exhausting state of being on high alert. 

Like I was saying earlier about the train getting delayed—when that happens, I don't suddenly start sweating and shaking and gasping for air. I just sit there and quietly let my brain run through every single way my career could end because of a ten-minute transit delay. So, yeah, the fear is definitely intense, but it's not a sudden episode. It's just my whole day. Every day. It never really peaks because it never really drops, if that makes sense? I'm just always living at that baseline of being completely overwhelmed by it all.

They were totally panic tears, not sad tears. \n\nSo, no, I don't walk around feeling sad or hopeless. I'm just so desperately trying to keep all the plates spinning and make sure nothing goes wrong, which is exhausting, obviously, like I was saying before, but it's definitely a totally different feeling than being depressed. I care way too much to be hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a variety of everyday things, even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned about them?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It is... it’s just this never-ending carousel in my head, all day, every day. I mean, it’s exactly like you said—there usually isn't even a real, logical reason for me to be panicking, but my mind just completely runs away with things anyway. \n\nIt’s like my brain just keeps jumping from one worst-case scenario to the next without giving me a second to breathe. Like, I’ll start out worrying about whether I’m falling behind at work—which, you know, I'm always terrified of, even if my manager literally just told me I’m doing a great job and I'm totally on schedule. But I can't just accept that. Instead, my brain immediately pivots, and suddenly I'm thinking, \"Well, if I'm struggling to keep up with work, then why am I spending all this time and money on these extra professional development courses?\" And then I'm spiraling about whether I’m just completely wasting my money on school, and how I'm going to ruin my finances, and what if I can't pay my rent. \n\nAnd then, before I can even try to rationalize *that*, I’m suddenly stressing about my family. I'll picture them looking at me with this intense disappointment, thinking I'm this helpless adult who can't handle basic responsibilities—even though they have literally never said anything like that to me, ever! An unread email, a slightly weird tone in a text message, a minor typo in a draft—I somehow twist it in my head until it means I'm going to lose my job, go broke, and alienate my entire family. I just can't seem to stop connecting these tiny, everyday things to the absolute ruin of my life, no matter how much I try to tell myself I'm being irrational.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that peaks within minutes, along with physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling short of breath?\n\nPatient: Sudden episodes? Um... no, I wouldn't say they're sudden. And they definitely don't just peak in a few minutes and then go away. It's really not like that for me at all. \n\nI think I know exactly what you're talking about, though, because I had a roommate in college who used to get those. She would just suddenly be terrified out of nowhere, and her heart would be racing so fast she thought she was having a heart attack, and she'd be sweating and hyperventilating. I would have to sit with her on the floor and help her count her breaths until it passed. But I don't experience anything like that. My fear isn't this sudden, out-of-the-blue attack that hits me like a wave and then recedes. It's more like... it's just always there. It's a constant, steady hum of dread that starts the second I open my eyes in the morning and doesn't stop until I finally manage to fall asleep. \n\nSo I don't get those sudden physical things you mentioned, like the sweating or the shortness of breath or the trembling. I mean, sure, my heart might beat a little fast sometimes when I'm really, really spiraling about a project deadline or if I have to present a big data analysis to the senior management team, but it's not like a sudden, intense spike where I can't catch my breath or anything. It's just this continuous, exhausting state of being on high alert. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about the train getting delayed—when that happens, I don't suddenly start sweating and shaking and gasping for air. I just sit there and quietly let my brain run through every single way my career could end because of a ten-minute transit delay. So, yeah, the fear is definitely intense, but it's not a sudden episode. It's just my whole day. Every day. It never really peaks because it never really drops, if that makes sense? I'm just always living at that baseline of being completely overwhelmed by it all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like, a couple of weeks ago, I actually burst into tears in my car in the office parking garage before going inside. But I wasn't crying because I was sad! I was crying because I had triple-checked this massive quarterly report the night before and I was absolutely convinced I had still missed some catastrophic error that was going to get me fired as soon as I walked through the doors. They were totally panic tears, not sad tears. \n\nSo, no, I don't walk around feeling sad or hopeless. I'm just so desperately trying to keep all the plates spinning and make sure nothing goes wrong, which is exhausting, obviously, like I was saying before, but it's definitely a totally different feeling than being depressed. I care way too much to be hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a variety of everyday things, even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned about them?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It is... it’s just this never-ending carousel in my head, all day, every day. I mean, it’s exactly like you said—there usually isn't even a real, logical reason for me to be panicking, but my mind just completely runs away with things anyway. \n\nIt’s like my brain just keeps jumping from one worst-case scenario to the next without giving me a second to breathe. Like, I’ll start out worrying about whether I’m falling behind at work—which, you know, I'm always terrified of, even if my manager literally just told me I’m doing a great job and I'm totally on schedule. But I can't just accept that. Instead, my brain immediately pivots, and suddenly I'm thinking, \"Well, if I'm struggling to keep up with work, then why am I spending all this time and money on these extra professional development courses?\" And then I'm spiraling about whether I’m just completely wasting my money on school, and how I'm going to ruin my finances, and what if I can't pay my rent. \n\nAnd then, before I can even try to rationalize *that*, I’m suddenly stressing about my family. I'll start thinking about how if I mess up at work and waste all my savings, my parents are going to find out and they're going to think I just completely lack the ability to handle my own life. I'll picture them looking at me with this intense disappointment, thinking I'm this helpless adult who can't handle basic responsibilities—even though they have literally never said anything like that to me, ever! They're actually really supportive, but my brain just invents this narrative where they think I'm a total failure. \n\nIt’s honestly exhausting because it happens with everything. It’s not just the big stuff. It’s like... I treat every single small, insignificant issue like it is this massive, catastrophic event that could completely derail my whole future. An unread email, a slightly weird tone in a text message, a minor typo in a draft—I somehow twist it in my head until it means I'm going to lose my job, go broke, and alienate my entire family. I just can't seem to stop connecting these tiny, everyday things to the absolute ruin of my life, no matter how much I try to tell myself I'm being irrational.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that peaks within minutes, along with physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling short of breath?\n\nPatient: Sudden episodes? Um... no, I wouldn't say they're sudden. And they definitely don't just peak in a few minutes and then go away. It's really not like that for me at all. \n\nI think I know exactly what you're talking about, though, because I had a roommate in college who used to get those. She would just suddenly be terrified out of nowhere, and her heart would be racing so fast she thought she was having a heart attack, and she'd be sweating and hyperventilating. I would have to sit with her on the floor and help her count her breaths until it passed. But I don't experience anything like that. My fear isn't this sudden, out-of-the-blue attack that hits me like a wave and then recedes. It's more like... it's just always there. It's a constant, steady hum of dread that starts the second I open my eyes in the morning and doesn't stop until I finally manage to fall asleep. \n\nSo I don't get those sudden physical things you mentioned, like the sweating or the shortness of breath or the trembling. I just sit there and quietly let my brain run through every single way my career could end because of a ten-minute transit delay. So, yeah, the fear is definitely intense, but it's not a sudden episode. It's just my whole day. Every day. It never really peaks because it never really drops, if that makes sense? I'm just always living at that baseline of being completely overwhelmed by it all.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that you worry about a number of different things on most days, rather than just specific events or situations?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. It's just this continuous, exhausting state of being on high alert. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about the train getting delayed—when that happens, I don't suddenly start sweating and shaking and gasping for air. I just sit there and quietly let my brain run through every single way my career could end because of a ten-minute transit delay. So, yeah, the fear is definitely intense, but it's not a sudden episode. It's just my whole day. Every day. It never really peaks because it never really drops, if that makes sense? I'm just always living at that baseline of being completely overwhelmed by it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about a number of different things on most days, rather than just specific events or situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, like I was kind of saying before... it’s definitely not just tied to specific events. I mean, if I have a huge data analysis due for the senior partners, obviously I’ll worry about that, but this is different. This is every single day, no matter what is actually happening in my life. It’s like my brain just has this rotating Rolodex of entirely different things to panic about, and it just never stops spinning. \n\nI really don't need a clear situation or a specific trigger to set me off. My mind just keeps jumping from one completely unrelated thing to the next. Like I mentioned earlier, I'll just be sitting at my desk, and I'll start stressing over whether I’m falling behind at work, and then, without any logical transition at all, it just shifts to whether I’m completely wasting my money on school and ruining my financial stability. And then, you know, five minutes later, I'm spiraling about my family and worrying that they're going to think I just can’t handle my life and that I'm a complete mess. \n\nAnd this happens on most days—honestly, pretty much every day. It’s never just one specific situation that I can pinpoint and say, \"Okay, I'm worried because of this one concrete thing.\" It’s more like... I take every single small, random issue, no matter how tiny, and I treat it like it’s this massive, catastrophic thing that could completely derail my whole future. I could literally just get a slightly vague email from a client, or notice a typo in a spreadsheet, and my brain instantly turns it into a web of totally different, life-ruining scenarios. So, yeah, it's definitely a lot of different things all at once, all the time, rather than just one specific event."
